3\to 3
, the sum of
3\to 1
and
1\to 3
and finally
1\to 1
transitions, mediated by an intermediate three-Reggeon state. Each term is evaluated in turn using the results of sections
4.1
,
4.2
and
4.3
, as discussed below.

The
\mathbf{3\to 3}
transition
involves three contributions, given on the right-hand side of

eq. (
4.15
). The first term yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\pi^{2}}{\alpha_{s}^{4}r_{\Gamma}^{4}}\langle\psi_{j,3}|H_{3\to 3}^{2}|\psi_{i,3}\rangle&=\frac{ig^{2}_{s}}{t}\Bigg[\left(2J_{7}-\frac{J_{5}}{2}-J_{6}-J_{8}\right)\mathbf{C}_{\text{DL}}-\left(3J_{6}-2J_{7}-J_{8}+J_{9}\right)\mathbf{C}_{\text{ML}}\\
&+\left({J_{4}+2J_{6}-J_{7}-\frac{J_{8}}{2}}\right)\,C_{A}\,\mathbf{C}^{(3)}_{33}-\frac{3}{4}\left(J_{4}+2J_{6}\right)\,C_{A}^{2}\,\mathbf{C}^{(2)}_{33}\Bigg],\end{split}

(5.23)

where the factors multiplying
\mathbf{C}_{\text{DL}}
and
\mathbf{C}_{\text{ML}}
in the first line arise from the integrals in eqs. (
4.18a
) and (
4.18b
), respectively; the contribution proportional to
\mathbf{C}^{(3)}_{33}
, in the second line, is obtained by integrating eq. (
4.20a
) and the last term in eq. (
5.23
) corresponds to eq. (
4.20b
). The remaining terms in eq. (
4.15
), defined in eqs. (
4.21a
) and (
4.21b
), yield

\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{2}}{\alpha_{s}^{4}r_{\Gamma}^{4}}\Bigg[2\alpha_{g}(t)\,\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\,\langle\psi_{j,3}|H_{3\to 3}|\psi_{i,3}\rangle\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{ig^{2}_{s}}{t}\Bigg[\left(J_{3}-2J_{2}\right)\,\mathbf{T}^{2}_{t}\mathbf{C}^{(3)}_{33}+\frac{3J_{2}}{2}C_{A}\,\mathbf{C}^{(3)}_{33}\Bigg],

(5.24a)

\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{2}}{\alpha_{s}^{4}r_{\Gamma}^{4}}\,\alpha_{g}^{2}(t)\,\left(\mathbf{T}^{2}_{t}\right)^{2}\,\langle\psi_{j,3}|\psi_{i,3}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{ig_{s}^{2}}{t}\,\frac{J_{1}}{4}\left(\mathbf{T}^{2}_{t}\right)^{2}\mathbf{C}^{(2)}_{33}.

(5.24b)

The
3\to 3
transition amplitude is obtained by combining eqs. (
5.23
), (
5.24a
) and (
5.24b
). The relevant colour factors are given in eqs. (
4.33
), (
4.36
), (
4.49a
) and (
4.49b
). Furthermore, we make use of the three- and four-loop identities in eq. (
4.45
) and (
4.48
) to simplify the colour structures and to isolate the leading contributions in the planar limit. We find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle j_{3}|\tilde{H}_{3\to 3}^{2}|i_{3}\rangle&=\Bigg[\frac{d_{AA}}{72\,N_{A}}\Big(3J_{4}+J_{5}-J_{6}-2J_{7}-J_{9}\Big)\\
&+\frac{C_{A}^{4}}{1728}\Big(3J_{1}+6J_{2}-12J_{3}+5J_{5}+J_{6}-10J_{7}+7J_{9}\Big)\\
&-\frac{1}{32}{\Big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\Big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}}\,\Big(4J_{4}-3J_{6}+2J_{7}-3J_{9}\Big)\\
&+\frac{1}{96}{\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\left[\left(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\right)^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right]}\,\Big(J_{1}-4J_{2}+2J_{3}+2J_{4}+J_{5}+2J_{6}-4J_{7}\Big)\\
&-\frac{1}{32}{\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}}\,\Big(3J_{4}+J_{5}-J_{6}-2J_{7}-J_{9}\Big)\\
&+\frac{1}{32}{\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}}\Big(J_{1}+2J_{2}-4J_{3}+3J_{4}-4J_{6}+4J_{7}-2J_{9}\Big)\\
&-\frac{C_{A}}{24}\left(\frac{d_{AR_{i}}}{N_{R_{i}}C_{i}}+\frac{d_{AR_{j}}}{N_{R_{j}}C_{j}}\right)\Big(J_{1}+2J_{2}-4J_{3}+J_{4}+2J_{5}-4J_{7}+2J_{9}\Big)\Bigg]\,\langle j_{1}|i_{1}\rangle.\end{split}

(5.25)

Using section
4.3
we substitute the results for the integrals
J_{1}\dots J_{9}
, expanded in powers of
\epsilon
, into eq. (
5.25
), getting

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle j_{3}|\tilde{H}_{3\to 3}^{2}|i_{3}\rangle&=\Bigg[\frac{\mathbf{C}^{(4,-4)}}{\epsilon^{4}}+\frac{\hat{\zeta}_{3}}{\epsilon}\,\mathbf{C}^{(4,-1)}+{\cal{O}}\left(\epsilon\right)\Bigg]\,\langle j_{1}|i_{1}\rangle,\end{split}

(5.26)

where we have defined the following operators in colour space

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbf{C}^{(4,-4)}=\frac{1}{432}\left[\frac{d_{AA}}{N_{A}}-3C_{A}\left(\frac{d_{AR_{i}}}{N_{R_{i}}C_{i}}+\frac{d_{AR_{j}}}{N_{R_{j}}C_{j}}\right)+\frac{C_{A}^{4}}{12}\right]-\frac{1}{192}[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\\
&\phantom{\mathbf{C}^{(4,-4)}=}+\frac{1}{96}\Big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\Big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}+\frac{7}{576}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\left[\left(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\right)^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right]-\frac{5}{192}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\end{split}

(5.27a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbf{C}^{(4,-1)}=-\frac{101}{216}\left[\frac{d_{AA}}{N_{A}}-\frac{312}{101}C_{A}\left(\frac{d_{AR_{i}}}{N_{R_{i}}C_{i}}+\frac{d_{AR_{j}}}{N_{R_{j}}C_{j}}\right)+\frac{211C_{A}^{4}}{2424}\right]+\frac{101}{96}\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\\
&\phantom{\mathbf{C}^{(4,-1)}=}+\frac{49}{48}\Big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\Big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}-\frac{47}{288}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\,\left[\left(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\right)^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right]-\frac{49}{48}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\left[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}.\end{split}

(5.27b)

The terms in square brackets in eqs. (
5.27a
) and (
5.27b
) are leading in the planar limit, while all other terms, involving commutators of
\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}
and
\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}
, are suppressed for large
N_{c}
.

The
\mathbf{3\to 1}
and
\mathbf{1\to 3}
transitions
, defined in eq. (
4.22a
), are computed by summing eqs. (
4.24
), (
4.26
) and (
4.27
), together with their symmetric contributions under exchange of the target and the projectile. The latter are obtained from the former by modifying the colour factors, as explained below eq. (
4.27
). Therefore, summing each expression in eqs. (
4.24
), (
4.26
) and (
4.27
), with its target-projectile symmetric partner, we obtain, respectively, the following compact results:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\pi^{2}}{\alpha_{s}^{4}\,r_{\Gamma}^{4}}\Big[\langle\psi_{j,3}|B_{3\to 3}H_{1\to 3}|\psi_{i,1}\rangle+\langle\psi_{j,1}|H_{3\to 1}B_{3\to 3}|\psi_{i,3}\rangle\Big]&\\
&\hskip-170.71652pt=i\frac{g_{s}^{2}}{t}\,\frac{\mathbf{C}^{(4),A}_{31}+\mathbf{C}^{(4),A}_{13}}{12}\Big[2J_{2}-J_{3}+J_{5}+J_{6}-4J_{7}+J_{9}\Big],\end{split}

(5.28a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\pi^{2}}{\alpha_{s}^{4}r_{\Gamma}^{4}}\left[\langle\psi_{j,1}|H_{3\to 1}\,A_{3\to 3}|\psi_{i,3}\rangle+\langle\psi_{j,3}|A_{3\to 3}\,H_{1\to 3}|\psi_{i,1}\rangle\right]&\\
&\hskip-170.71652pt=-\frac{ig_{s}^{2}}{t}\frac{C_{A}\left(\mathbf{C}^{(3)}_{13}+\mathbf{C}^{(3)}_{31}\right)}{24}\Big[3J_{2}+J_{4}+2J_{6}-4J_{7}-2J_{8}\Big],\end{split}

(5.28b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\pi^{2}\alpha_{g}(t)}{\alpha_{s}^{4}r_{\Gamma}^{4}}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\left[\langle\psi_{j,1}|H_{3\to 1}|\psi_{i,3}\rangle+\langle\psi_{j,3}|H_{1\to 3}|\psi_{i,1}\rangle\right]&\\
&\hskip-85.35826pt=\frac{ig_{s}^{2}}{t}\frac{\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\,\left(\mathbf{C}^{(3)}_{13}+\mathbf{C}^{(3)}_{31}\right)}{24}\Big[J_{1}+J_{2}-2J_{3}\Big]\,.\end{split}

(5.28c)

Next, summing eqs. (
5.28a
), (
5.28b
) and (
5.28c
), and using the expressions in eqs. (
4.44
) and (
4.54
) for the colour factors, we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle j_{1}|\tilde{H}_{3\to 1}\tilde{H}_{3\to 3}|i_{3}\rangle&+\langle j_{3}|\tilde{H}_{3\to 3}\tilde{H}_{1\to 3}|i_{1}\rangle=\\
&=\frac{C_{A}}{24}\left(\frac{d_{AR_{i}}}{N_{R_{i}}C_{i}}+\frac{d_{AR_{j}}}{N_{R_{j}}C_{j}}\right)(J_{1}+2J_{2}-4J_{3}+J_{4}+2J_{5}-4J_{7}+2J_{9})\,\\
&=\frac{C_{A}}{144}\left(\frac{d_{AR_{i}}}{N_{R_{i}}C_{i}}+\frac{d_{AR_{j}}}{N_{R_{j}}C_{j}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{4}}-\frac{208\hat{\zeta}_{3}}{\epsilon}+{\cal{O}}\left(\epsilon\right)\right)\langle j_{1}|i_{1}\rangle\,,\end{split}

(5.29)

where in the second line we expanded the integrals in
\epsilon
using the results in section
4.3
.
We notice that the first line of eq. (
5.29
) is just the negative of the terms proportional to quartic Casimirs
d_{AR_{i}}
and
d_{AR_{j}}
in the
3\to 3
transition, eq. (
5.25
). Therefore, in the four-loop reduced amplitude, eq. (
5.22
), these contributions will cancel exactly to all orders in
\epsilon
, as in the case of the three-loop transition amplitudes, eq. (
5.17
) and (
5.19
).

The
\mathbf{1\to 1}
transition, mediated by three-Reggeon states
, defined in eq. (
4.29
), involves two contributions, given in eqs. (
4.30a
) and (
4.30b
). After performing the relevant integrals of the kernel
H_{13}
, we obtain

\frac{\pi^{2}}{\alpha_{s}^{4}r_{\Gamma}^{4}}\langle\psi_{j,1}|H_{3\to 1}H_{1\to 3}|\psi_{i,1}\rangle_{D}=-\frac{ig_{s}^{2}}{t}\frac{\mathbf{C}^{(4),D}_{3113}}{144}\,\left[J_{1}+2J_{2}-4J_{3}+J_{4}+2\left(J_{5}-2J_{7}+J_{9}\right)\right]

(5.30a)

\frac{\pi^{2}}{\alpha_{s}^{4}r_{\Gamma}^{4}}\langle\psi_{j,1}|H_{3\to 1}H_{1\to 3}|\psi_{i,1}\rangle_{X}=-\frac{ig_{s}^{2}}{t}\frac{\mathbf{C}^{(4),X}_{3113}}{144}\left[J_{1}+2J_{2}-4J_{3}+J_{5}+J_{6}-2\left(J_{7}+J_{8}\right)+3J_{9}\right]

(5.30b)

By substituting the colour factors
\mathbf{C}^{(4),D}_{3113}
and
\mathbf{C}^{(4),X}_{3113}
given in eqs. (
4.60a
) and (
4.60b
), respectively, we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle j_{1}|\tilde{H}_{3\to 1}\tilde{H}_{1\to 3}|i_{1}\rangle&=-\frac{1}{72}\Bigg[\frac{d_{AA}}{N_{A}}(3J_{1}+6J_{2}-12J_{3}-3J_{4}+4J_{5}+2J_{6}-8J_{7}+8J_{9})\\
&\phantom{-\frac{1}{72}\Bigg[}+\frac{C_{A}^{4}}{12}(3J_{4}+J_{5}-J_{6}-2J_{7}-J_{9})\Bigg]\langle j_{1}|i_{1}\rangle.\end{split}

(5.31)

We expand the integrals
J_{1}\dots J_{9}
, finding

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle j_{1}|\tilde{H}_{3\to 1}\tilde{H}_{1\to 3}|i_{1}\rangle&=\Bigg[-\frac{1}{432\epsilon^{4}}\left(\frac{d_{AA}}{N_{A}}+\frac{C_{A}^{4}}{12}\right)+\frac{55\,\hat{\zeta}_{3}}{108\epsilon}\left(\frac{d_{AA}}{N_{A}}+\frac{101C_{A}^{4}}{1320}\right)\Bigg]\langle j_{1}|i_{1}\rangle.\end{split}

(5.32)

The
1\to 1
transition amplitude mediated by three-Reggeon states in eq. (
5.32
) has the same colour structure of the tree-level amplitude,
\langle j_{1}|i_{1}\rangle
. Furthermore, the result is independent on the representation of the colliding particles and it depends only on the degrees of freedom of the exchanged Reggeized gluons, through the Casimir invariants
C_{A}
and
d_{AA}
.

The four-loop reduced amplitude
is given by the sum in eq. (
5.22
) of the
3\to 3
,
1\to 3
and
3\to 1
, and
1\to 3\to 1
transition amplitudes. Using the corresponding expressions in eqs. (
5.25
), (
5.29
) and (
5.32
), respectively, we get

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,4,2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}r_{\Gamma}^{4}}{2}\Bigg\{-\frac{1}{24}\left(\frac{d_{AA}}{N_{A}}-\frac{C_{A}^{4}}{24}\right)(J_{1}+2J_{2}-4J_{3}-2J_{4}+J_{5}+J_{6}-2J_{7}+3J_{9})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{32}{\Big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\Big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}}\,\Big(4J_{4}-3J_{6}+2J_{7}-3J_{9}\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{96}{\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\left[\left(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\right)^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right]}\,\Big(J_{1}-4J_{2}+2J_{3}+2J_{4}+J_{5}+2J_{6}-4J_{7}\Big)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{32}{\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}}\,\Big(3J_{4}+J_{5}-J_{6}-2J_{7}-J_{9}\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{32}{\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}}\Big(J_{1}+2J_{2}-4J_{3}+3J_{4}-4J_{6}+4J_{7}-2J_{9}\Big)\Bigg\}\,\langle j_{1}|i_{1}\rangle.

(5.33)

Here we notice some important aspects of the reduced amplitude. As anticipated, all terms arising from
3\to 1
and
1\to 3
transitions, eq. (
5.29
), which are proportional to the quartic Casimir invariants
d_{AR_{i}}
and
d_{AR_{j}}
, cancel in the sum with the
3\to 3
amplitude, eq. (
5.25
) exactly, to all orders in
\epsilon
. This cancellation has already been observed in the three-loop reduced amplitude, in eq. (
5.19
). Based on this observation, we conjecture that transition amplitudes connecting three-Reggeon states with a single Reggeon cancel in the reduced amplitude
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-),\text{NNLL}}
to all perturbative orders. More precisely, we expect a cancellation in eq. (
3.59
) between the quartic Casimir contributions associated with the projectile and the target inside
\langle j_{3}|\tilde{H}_{3\to 3}^{l}|i_{3}\rangle
and the entire contribution due to
\langle j_{1}|\tilde{H}_{3\to 1}\tilde{H}_{3\to 3}^{l}|i_{3}\rangle
and
\langle j_{3}|\tilde{H}_{3\to 3}^{l}\tilde{H}_{1\to 3}|i_{1}\rangle
, for all
l
.

The first line in eq. (
5.3
) is proportional to the colour factor
\frac{d_{AA}}{N_{A}}-\frac{C_{A}^{4}}{24}
, which emerges from the sum of eqs. (
5.25
) and (
5.32
). These are indeed the only contributions that involve
d_{AA}
and
C_{A}^{4}
. Interestingly, this combination is non-planar, as it can be easily checked by using the expression of
d_{AA}
in
\mathrm{SU}(N_{c})
, given in eq. (
4.42
)

\frac{d_{AA}}{N_{A}}-\frac{C_{A}^{4}}{24}=0\cdot N_{c}^{4}+\frac{3}{2}N_{c}^{2}.

(5.34)

All remaining terms in eq. (
5.3
) are manifestly non-planar, because they are written in terms of commutators of
\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}
and
\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}
. This guarantees that the four-loop reduced amplitude is non-planar as a whole. Notably, all planar contributions in the
3\to 3
transition, which are proportional to the colour factors {
d_{AR_{i}}
,
d_{AR_{j}}
} and {
d_{AA}
,
C_{A}^{4}
}, are cancelled exactly by the
1\to 3
and
3\to 1
transitions, and by the
1\to 3\to 1
transition, respectively, to all orders in
\epsilon
. This mechanism is in place starting at four loops, where all transitions amplitudes are available. At two and at three loops, there remain planar terms in the reduced amplitudes, which are associated to the
3\to 3
transitions in eq. (
5.5
) and (
5.17
), respectively.

We expand the result in eq. (
5.3
) using the
J_{i}
integrals in section
4.3
and obtain

\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,4,2)}=\frac{\pi^{2}\,r^{4}_{\Gamma}}{2}\Bigg[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{4}}\mathbf{K}^{(4)}+\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\zeta_{3}+\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{4}\right)\mathbf{K}^{(1)}+{\cal O}(\epsilon)\Bigg]\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}},

(5.35)

where we replaced
\hat{\zeta}_{3}
using eq. (
4.71
) to explicitly display the resulting
{\cal O}(\epsilon^{0})
contribution. This result is exact in as far as divergent and finite contributions to
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,4,2)}
are concerned. Higher orders in the
\epsilon
expansion, which we suppressed here, can be readily obtained from eq. (
5.3
) using the integrals in section
4.3
. The colour structures in eq. (
5.35
) are defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{K}^{(4)}&=\frac{1}{96}\Big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\Big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}+\frac{7}{576}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\left[\left(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\right)^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right]\\
&-\frac{1}{192}\left[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}-\frac{5}{192}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\left[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\end{split}

(5.36a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{K}^{(1)}&=\frac{49}{48}\Big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\Big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}-\frac{47}{288}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\left[\left(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\right)^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right]\\
&+\frac{101}{96}\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}-\frac{49}{48}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\big[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}+\frac{1}{24}\left(\frac{d_{AA}}{N_{A}}-\frac{C_{A}^{4}}{24}\right)\,.\end{split}

(5.36b)

The colour tensors in eqs. (
5.36a
) and (
5.36b
) have a transparent interpretation in the large-
N_{c}
limit. However, different choices of colour bases can also be considered. For instance, ref.
Naculich:2020clm
proposes a basis of nested commutators, which allows one to uncover all-orders relations between the Regge limits of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM and
\mathcal{N}=8
supergravity amplitudes, and verifies that such basis is suitable to write the three-loop
\mathcal{N}=4
amplitude of ref.
Henn:2016jdu
. We checked that also the four-loop amplitude in eq. (
5.35
) can be expressed in the basis of ref.
Naculich:2020clm
, by computing explicitly eqs. (
5.36a
) and (
5.36b
) in the adjoint representation.

Explicit results of the four-loop reduced amplitude, eq. (
5.35
), expanded in
\epsilon
and evaluated in
t
-channel colour basis are presented in appendix
D
. Specifically, the octet and the singlet components of the quark-quark reduced amplitudes are presented, respectively, in eqs. (
D.3a
) and (
D.4c
). The antisymmetric octet and the decuplet of the gluon-gluon amplitude are given in eqs. (
D.3b
) and (
D.5c
), respectively. The antisymmetric octet component of the quark-gluon amplitude is reported in eq. (
D.3c
).

5.4
Disentangling the pole and cut contributions

We have been computing amplitudes in the MRS scheme of eq. (
2.38
), where we have separated the contributions from transitions mediated purely by single-Reggeon states from those involving multiple-Reggeon states, which we explicitly computed to four loops. In this section we will show that by redefining the two-loop impact factors
C_{i}^{(2)}
and the three-loop Regge trajectory
\alpha_{g}^{(3)}
, we can absorb the entire set of planar corrections at two and three loops arising in the multi-Reggeon exchanges of eqs. (
5.12
) and (
5.20
) respectively, into a Regge-pole factorising term, thus rendering the remaining term, which is associated with the Regge cut, non-planar. This implements the Regge-cut scheme of eq. (
2.39
).

The fact that this transformation can be done is important and non-trivial. It requires in particular that the planar MRS corrections would themselves be process-independent. In fact, we already know that this is the case, see eqs. (
5.14
) and (
5.21
) above.
To expose the different contributions explicitly, we will compute the full NNLL odd amplitude at two loops, three loops and four loops for
qq
,
gg
and
qg
scattering. We begin with the decomposition in eq. (
2.38
), which we expand in the coupling and the high-energy logarithm as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(-)}_{ij\to ij}=&\,e^{C_{A}\alpha_{g}(t)L}\,C_{i}(t)\,C_{j}(t)\,{\cal M}^{\rm tree}_{ij\to ij}+\mathcal{M}^{(-),\text{MRS}}_{ij\to ij}\\
\equiv&\sum_{m,n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{m}L^{n}\left[p_{ij}^{(m,n)}c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}+(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{m}f_{ij}^{(m,n)}\right]\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree},[8_{a}]},\end{split}

(5.37)

where we define the functions
p_{ij}^{(m,n)}
and
f_{ij}^{(m,n)}
for the SRS and MRS parts respectively, and where the tree amplitude with the octet colour tensor
c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}
stripped off,
\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree},[8_{a}]}
, is defined in eq. (
2.13
). Note that
f_{ij}^{(m,n)}
is a linear combination of colour tensors (depending on the representations of the scattered partons
i
and
j
) while from
p_{ij}^{(m,n)}
we have extracted the colour tensor structure, so as to emphasise the fact that the Regge pole contributes only to the
c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}
component and does not involve other colour components.
The impact factors
C_{i/j}(t)
are assumed to be evaluated at the renormalisation scale
\mu^{2}=-t
. We utilise the orthornormal
t
-channel basis of refs.
DelDuca:2014cya
;
Caron-Huot:2017fxr
, given in eq. (
2.15
) for the relevant colour tensors.

The function
p_{ij}^{(m,n)}
can be found from expanding the exponential in eq. (
5.37
)
where the quark and gluon impact factors and gluon Regge trajectory in QCD are given in appendix
A
,
while
f_{ij}^{(m,n)}
can be found from the reduced amplitudes given in sections
5.1
through
5.3
. Using eqs. (
2.44
), (
2.45
) and (
5.37
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(-),\text{MRS}}_{ij\to ij}&=\,(i\pi)^{2}\sum_{m=2}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}r_{\Gamma}\right)^{m}\sum_{n=0}^{m-2}L^{n}f_{ij}^{(m,n)}\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree},[8_{a}]}\\
&=Z_{i}(t)Z_{j}(t)e^{\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\alpha_{g}(t)L}\left(\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-)}_{ij\to ij}-D_{i}(t)D_{j}(t)\mathcal{M}^{\text{tree}}_{ij\to ij}\right),\end{split}

(5.38)

where we indicated that the expansion starts at two loops and that the highest logarithms are NNLL, with
n=m-2
. The combination in parentheses in the second line is the
\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{ij\to ij}^{(-),\,\rm MRS}
of eq. (
2.45
). We now evaluate
p_{ij}
and
f_{ij}
at NNLL accuracy at two, three and four loops in the MRS scheme. Subsequently, we will use these values to compute the corresponding coefficients in the cut scheme of eq. (
2.39
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(-)}_{ij\to ij}=&\,e^{C_{A}\tilde{\alpha}_{g}(t)L}\,\tilde{C}_{i}(t)\,\tilde{C}_{j}(t)\,{\cal M}^{\rm tree}_{ij\to ij}+\mathcal{M}^{(-),\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}\\
\equiv&\sum_{m,n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{m}L^{n}\left[\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(m,n)}c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}+(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{m}\tilde{f}_{ij}^{(m,n)}\right]\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree},[8_{a}]}\,,\end{split}

(5.39)

where
\tilde{f}_{ij}
is related to
{f}_{ij}
by simply removing the planar
8_{a}
component (there is no change of the remaining components). Of course, the sum of terms in the square brackets in eq. (
5.39
) must exactly match that in eq. (
5.37
), that is, the relation between the NNLL coefficients in the MRS and cut schemes is consistent with

(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{m}\Big(\tilde{f}_{ij}^{(m,m-2)}-{f}_{ij}^{(m,m-2)}\Big)=-\Big(\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(m,m-2)}-{p}_{ij}^{(m,m-2)}\Big)c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}\,.

(5.40)

In the following we shall see that the freedom to make such shifts exists only for two and three loops (
m=2,3
) corresponding respectively to fixing the impact factor
\tilde{C}_{i}^{(2)}
and the three-loop Regge trajectory
\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}
. At four loop and beyond there are no parameters that could be tuned at NNLL, and given that (on general grounds
Mandelstam:1963cw
;
Eden:1966dnq
;
Collins:1977jy
) Regge cuts must be associated with non-planar diagrams, one should expect

{f}_{ij}^{(m,m-2)}
to be non-planar at four loops and beyond, and of course then
\tilde{f}_{ij}^{(m,m-2)}={f}_{ij}^{(m,m-2)}
for all
m\geq 4
. We shall see explicitly that this expectation is realised
at four loop: result of the calculation of
{f}_{ij}^{(4,2)}
is strictly non-planar.

One might also contemplate alternative schemes in which the difference in eq. (
5.40
) involves non-planar contributions beyond the planar ones. In what follows we shall see that at two loops there is indeed some freedom to do that, and absorb further non-planar corrections from the MRS component into the impact factors of the Regge pole.
This directly reproduces the scheme for the Regge cut proposed by Fadin and Lipatov in ref.
Fadin:2017nka
.
At three loops, in turn, there is no such freedom, since non-planar terms would be unnatural in
\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}
, which is expected to be computable as a correlator of cusped (lightlike) Wilson lines.
13
13

13

At two loops both the singular and finite parts of
{\alpha}_{g}(t)
(known from refs.
Fadin:1995xg
;
Fadin:1996tb
;
Fadin:1995km
;
Blumlein:1998ib
;
DelDuca:2001gu
) have been observed (see equation (1.6) of ref.
Falcioni:2019nxk
) to be related to the simple wedge configuration of two semi-infinite lightlike Wilson lines meeting at a cusp, which was computed to this order in refs.
Erdogan:2011yc
;
Falcioni:2019nxk
. Note that the finite part differs by a term proportional to
b_{0}\pi^{2}
, which is yet to be understood. Three-loop computations of this Wilson-line correlator are still lacking.
The non-abelian exponentiation theorem
Gatheral1983ExponentiationOE
;
Frenkel1984NonabelianEE
then implies a simple, maximally non-Abelian colour structure from any gluonic diagrams, with
(N_{c}\alpha_{s})^{m}
for
m=2,3
, respectively.

5.4.1
Two loops

At two loops we find the NNLL functions
p_{ij}^{(2,0)}
and
f_{ij}^{(2,0)}
as follows:
p_{ij}^{(2,0)}
is obtained from eq. (
5.37
) using the expansion of the impact factors defined in eq. (
2.43a
),

\displaystyle p_{ij}^{(2,0)}=

\displaystyle\,C_{i}^{(2)}+C_{j}^{(2)}+C_{i}^{(1)}C_{j}^{(1)}\,.

(5.41)

In turn,
f_{ij}^{(2,0)}
is obtained from eqs. (
5.38
) and (
5.12
):

\displaystyle(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}f_{ij}^{(2,0)}\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree},[8_{a}]}=

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,2,0)}-\Big(D_{i}^{(2)}+D_{j}^{(2)}+D_{i}^{(1)}D_{j}^{(1)}\Big)\,\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\pi^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}S^{(2)}(\epsilon)\left((\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2}-\frac{1}{12}C_{A}^{2}\right)\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree}}\,,

(5.42)

where
S^{(2)}(\epsilon)
is given in eq. (
5.13
).
The impact-factor coefficients
C_{i}^{(n)}
in eq. (
5.41
) are theory-dependent and can be found in QCD from the expressions for
D_{i}^{(n)}
and
Z_{i}^{(n)}
given in Appendix
A
. Here,
f_{ij}^{(2,0)}
is driven solely by the three-Reggeon exchange in eq. (
5.5
). Evaluating this function in the
t
-channel basis for different partonic processes, we have

\displaystyle f^{(2,0)}_{qq}=

\displaystyle\,-S^{(2)}(\epsilon)\bigg[\left(\frac{N_{c}^{2}}{6}-1+\frac{3}{N_{c}^{2}}\right)c_{qq}^{[8]}+\sqrt{N_{c}^{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{N_{c}^{2}}\right)c_{qq}^{[1]}\bigg],

(5.43a)

\displaystyle f^{(2,0)}_{gg}=

\displaystyle\,-S^{(2)}(\epsilon)\left[\left(\frac{N_{c}^{2}}{6}+6\right)c_{gg}^{[8_{a}]}+3\sqrt{\frac{N_{c}^{2}-4}{2}}c_{gg}^{[10+\bar{10}]}\right],

(5.43b)

\displaystyle f^{(2,0)}_{qg}=

\displaystyle\,-S^{(2)}(\epsilon)\left(\frac{N_{c}^{2}}{6}+1\right)c_{qg}^{[8_{a}]}\,,

(5.43c)

Now, it is evident that the leading-colour coefficient of
c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}
is the same for the different processes:

\left.f^{(2,0)}_{ij}\right|_{\text{planar}}=-\frac{N_{c}^{2}}{6}S^{(2)}(\epsilon)c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}=\frac{N_{c}^{2}}{6}\left(\frac{1}{8\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{3}{4}\zeta_{3}\epsilon-\frac{9}{8}\zeta_{4}\epsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\right)c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}\,,

(5.44)

which is in agreement with the universal result of
\left.\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,2,0)}\right|_{\text{planar}}
in eq. (
5.14
), as it must be. We then shift to the Regge-cut basis according to

\displaystyle\begin{split}(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}\tilde{f}^{(2,0)}_{ij}\equiv&\,(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}\left[f_{ij}^{(2,0)}+\frac{N_{c}^{2}}{6}S^{(2)}(\epsilon)c^{[8_{a}]}_{ij}\right]\\
=&(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}f_{ij}^{(2,0)}+\left(p_{ij}^{(2,0)}-\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(2,0)}\right)c^{[8_{a}]}_{ij}\,,\end{split}

(5.45)

where in the first line we defined
\tilde{f}^{(2,0)}_{ij}

by subtracting the planar part of
f_{ij}^{(2,0)}
using eq. (
5.44
) and in the second we
required a corresponding shift of
p_{ij}^{(2,0)}
using eq. (
5.40
).
To implement the latter we now recall eq. (
5.41
) and similarly expand the pole term in eq. (
5.39
) to two loops getting

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(2,0)}=

\displaystyle\,\tilde{C}_{i}^{(2)}+\tilde{C}_{j}^{(2)}+C_{i}^{(1)}C_{j}^{(1)}\,.

(5.46)

Note that since
\mathcal{M}^{(-),\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}
vanishes at NLL, the pole term is uniquely defined at that order and we have
\tilde{C}_{i/j}^{(1)}=C_{i/j}^{(1)}
. The required shift of the impact factor coefficient is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{C}_{i/j}^{(2)}\,=&\,C_{i/j}^{(2)}+N_{c}^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}\,\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\frac{1}{2}S^{(2)}(\epsilon)\\
\,=&\,C_{i/j}^{(2)}-N_{c}^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}\,\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{8\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{3}{4}\zeta_{3}\epsilon-\frac{9}{8}\zeta_{4}\epsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\right),\end{split}

(5.47)

where we retain the target-projectile symmetry by shifting
C_{i}^{(2)}
and
C_{j}^{(2)}
equally.

Our two-loop amplitude is then written in the Regge-cut scheme from eq. (
5.39
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(-,2,0)}_{ij\to ij}=&\left[\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(2,0)}c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}+(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}\tilde{f}^{(2,0)}_{ij}\right]\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree},[8_{a}]}\\
=&\left[\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(2,0)}+\pi^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}S^{(2)}(\epsilon)\left((\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2}-\frac{C_{A}^{2}}{4}\right)\right]\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree}}\end{split}

(5.48)

where we can then write

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(-,2,0),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}=\pi^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}S^{(2)}(\epsilon)\left((\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2}-\frac{C_{A}^{2}}{4}\right)\,\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree}}\,,

(5.49)

which can be easily verified to be non-planar by the identity in eq. (
5.11
). By evaluating the contribution to the octet of
\mathcal{M}^{(-,2,0),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}
for the scattering particles in the fundamental or the adjoint representations, we find agreement with the Regge-pole factorisation breaking term
R_{ij}^{(2),0,[8]}
, which was defined in refs.
DelDuca:2013ara
;
DelDuca:2014cya
based on considerations about the infrared singularities of the amplitudes rather than direct calculation.

Another proposed separation is that of Fadin and Lipatov in ref.
Fadin:2017nka
, which we will name the FL scheme. It can be achieved by not only absorbing the leading-colour component of
f_{ij}^{(2,0)}
into the Regge-pole term, but also the
\kappa(R_{i})
terms from the decomposition of
(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2}
in eq. (
5.11
). The impact factors are shifted from the MRS scheme by

\displaystyle C_{i}^{\text{FL},(2)}\,

\displaystyle=\,C_{i}^{(2)}+(r_{\Gamma})^{2}\,\pi^{2}\left(\frac{N_{c}^{2}}{12}+4\,\kappa(R_{i})\right)S^{(2)}(\epsilon)

(5.50)

and
C_{j}^{\text{FL},(2)}
can be found by replacing
C_{i}^{(2)}\to C_{j}^{(2)}
and
\kappa(R_{i})\to\kappa(R_{j})
above. Then the Regge-cut term in the FL scheme can be found to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(-,2,0),\,\text{FL-cut}}_{ij\to ij}=\pi^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}S^{(2)}(\epsilon)\left((\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2}-\frac{C_{A}^{2}}{4}-4\Big(\kappa(R_{i})+\kappa(R_{j})\Big)\right)\,\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree}}\,.

(5.51)

The colour structure of eq. (
5.51
) for the different processes aligns completely with the
C_{ij}^{C}
coefficients of ref.
Fadin:2017nka
. This can be checked using eq. (
5.43
) and the definition of the
\kappa(R_{i})
in eq. (
5.1
).
Upon using eq. (
5.11
) to substitute for
(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2}
in eq. (
5.51
) we see that the Regge-cut term in the FL scheme at two loops may be written directly in terms of commutators of
\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}
and
\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}
.

5.4.2
Three loops

At three loops, the SRS terms obtained from the expansion of eq. (
5.37
) read:

\displaystyle p_{ij}^{(3,1)}=

\displaystyle\,N_{c}\left[\alpha_{g}^{(3)}(t)+\alpha_{g}^{(2)}(t)\left(C_{i}^{(1)}+C_{j}^{(1)}\right)+\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\left(C_{i}^{(1)}C_{j}^{(1)}+C_{i}^{(2)}+C_{j}^{(2)}\right)\right],

(5.52)

while the MRS contribution is obtained from eq. (
5.38
) and using the expression from the first line of eq. (
5.42
) we have

\displaystyle(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{3}f_{ij}^{(3,1)}\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree},[8_{a}]}=

\displaystyle\,\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,3,1)}+\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}f_{ij}^{(2,0)}\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree},[8_{a}]},

(5.53)

where
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,3,1)}
is given by eq. (
5.20
).
Taking the planar part of the above equation and using eq. (
5.21
) we obtain (upon dropping an overall factor of
(i\pi)^{2}
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left.r_{\Gamma}^{3}f_{ij}^{(3,1)}\right|_{\text{planar}}=&\,r_{\Gamma}^{3}\,S_{C}^{(3)}(\epsilon)N_{c}^{3}\,c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}\,+\left.\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)N_{c}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}f_{ij}^{(2,0)}\right|_{\text{planar}}\,\\
=&\,r_{\Gamma}^{3}N_{c}^{3}\left[\,S_{C}^{(3)}(\epsilon)\,-\frac{1}{12\epsilon}S^{(2)}(\epsilon)\right]c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}\\
=&\,r_{\Gamma}^{3}N_{c}^{3}\left[\frac{1}{108\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{\zeta_{3}}{54}+\frac{\zeta_{4}}{36}\epsilon+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\right]c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}\,,\end{split}

(5.54)

where in the second line we used eq. (
5.44
) and in the third we substituted the explicit expansions of
S^{(2)}(\epsilon)
and
S_{C}^{(3)}(\epsilon)
from eqs. (
5.12
) and (
5.18c
), respectively.

As a check, let us evaluate
f^{(3,1)}_{ij}
for the different partonic processes,

\displaystyle f^{(3,1)}_{qq}=

\displaystyle\bigg[N_{c}^{3}\left(\frac{1}{108\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{\zeta_{3}}{54}+\frac{\zeta_{4}}{36}\epsilon\right)+N_{c}\left(-\frac{1}{12\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{11\zeta_{3}}{6}+\frac{11\zeta_{4}}{6}\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 125.19194pt+\frac{1}{N_{c}}\left(\frac{5}{24\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{31\zeta_{3}}{12}-\frac{31\zeta_{4}}{8}\epsilon\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\bigg]c_{qq}^{[8]}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sqrt{N_{c}^{2}-1}\frac{N_{c}^{2}-4}{N_{c}}\bigg[\left(\frac{1}{48\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{\zeta_{3}}{24}+\frac{\zeta_{4}}{16}\epsilon\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\bigg]c_{qq}^{[1]},

(5.55a)

\displaystyle f^{(3,1)}_{gg}=

\displaystyle\,\bigg[N_{c}^{3}\left(\frac{1}{108\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{\zeta_{3}}{54}+\frac{\zeta_{4}}{36}\epsilon\right)+N_{c}\left(\frac{1}{3\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{2\zeta_{3}}{3}+\zeta_{4}\epsilon\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\bigg]c_{gg}^{[8_{a}]}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sqrt{\frac{N_{c}^{2}-4}{2}}N_{c}\left(\frac{7}{24\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{65\zeta_{3}}{12}-\frac{65\zeta_{4}}{8}\epsilon+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\right)c_{gg}^{[10+\bar{10}]},

(5.55b)

\displaystyle f^{(3,1)}_{qg}=

\displaystyle\,\bigg[N_{c}^{3}\left(\frac{1}{108\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{\zeta_{3}}{54}+\frac{\zeta_{4}}{36}\epsilon\right)+N_{c}\left(\frac{1}{48\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{61\zeta_{3}}{24}+\frac{61\zeta_{4}}{16}\epsilon\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\bigg]c_{qg}^{[8_{a}]}.

(5.55c)

It is then clear that the leading-colour component of
c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}
is the same, consistently with the general result of eq. (
5.54
). Now, as before, we change to the Regge-cut scheme by expanding the pole term in eq. (
5.39
) to three loops

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(3,1)}=

\displaystyle\,N_{c}\left[\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}(t)+\alpha_{g}^{(2)}(t)\left(C_{i}^{(1)}+C_{j}^{(1)}\right)+\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\left(C_{i}^{(1)}C_{j}^{(1)}+\tilde{C}_{i}^{(2)}+\tilde{C}_{j}^{(2)}\right)\right].

(5.56)

Note that again, since the pole term at NLL is uniquely defined, we have
\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)}(t)=\alpha_{g}^{(2)}(t)
. The
\tilde{f}_{ij}^{(3,1)}
is found by subtracting the planar piece of eq. (
5.54
)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{ij}^{(3,1)}=

\displaystyle\,f_{ij}^{(3,1)}-\left.f_{ij}^{(3,1)}\right|_{\text{planar}}\,.

(5.57)

Using eq. (
5.40
) we then have

\displaystyle\begin{split}(i\pi)^{2}r_{\Gamma}^{3}\left.f_{ij}^{(3,1)}\right|_{\text{planar}}=&-\left(p_{ij}^{(3,1)}-\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(3,1)}\right)c^{[8_{a}]}_{ij}\\
=&-N_{c}\left[\alpha_{g}^{(3)}(t)-\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}(t)+\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\left(C_{i}^{(2)}-\tilde{C}_{i}^{(2)}+C_{j}^{(2)}-\tilde{C}_{j}^{(2)}\right)\right]c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}\,,\end{split}

(5.58)

where we used the expressions for
p_{ij}^{(3,1)}
and
\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(3,1)}
in eqs. (
5.52
) and (
5.56
), respectively. Substituting the value of the difference of the impact factors in eq. (
5.47
) we can see that the ensuing term directly cancels the
S^{(2)}(\epsilon)
term of
\left.f_{ij}^{(3,1)}\right|_{\text{planar}}
in eq. (
5.54
). Thus,
upon rearranging for
\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}(t)
, we find

\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}(t)=\,\alpha_{g}^{(3)}(t)-(r_{\Gamma})^{3}N_{c}^{2}\pi^{2}S_{C}^{(3)}(\epsilon).

(5.59)

We can evaluate
\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}(t)
explicitly in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM, as
\alpha_{g}^{(3)}(t)
in the MRS scheme was extracted in ref.
Caron-Huot:2017fxr
upon comparison to the three-loop four-gluon amplitude of ref.
Henn:2016jdu
. There it is found that

\alpha_{g}^{(3)}(t)|_{\text{SYM}}=N_{c}^{2}\left(-\frac{\zeta_{2}}{144}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{49\zeta_{4}}{192}\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\frac{107}{144}\zeta_{2}\zeta_{3}+\frac{\zeta_{5}}{4}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right).

(5.60)

Then, using eq. (
5.59
) and the value for
S_{C}^{(3)}(\epsilon)
in eq. (
5.18c
), we conclude that

\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}(t)\rvert_{\text{SYM}}=N_{c}^{2}\left(\frac{11}{48}\zeta_{4}\,\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,+\,\frac{5}{24}\zeta_{2}\zeta_{3}+\frac{\zeta_{5}}{4}\,+\,\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right).

(5.61)

As expected from the discussion around eq. (
2.40
), this new definition of the Regge trajectory in the Regge-cut scheme has the same singularity structure as
K^{(3)}
, the integral of the three-loop coefficient of the lightlike cusp anomalous dimension. This can be verified when one substitutes the maximal transcendental part of
\gamma_{K}^{(3)}=\frac{11}{4}\zeta_{4}
from eq. (
B
) into
K^{(3)}
of eq. (
A.2a
), remembering that the beta function coefficients vanishes in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM.
It is in complete agreement with the result from the planar theory
Drummond:2007aua
;
Naculich:2007ub
;
DelDuca:2008jg
, in line with the expectation that it is an eikonal quantity, featuring only maximally non-Abelian colour structures.

Let us also express the three-loop amplitude in the Regge-cut scheme as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(-,3,1)}_{ij\to ij}=

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(3,1)}c_{ij}^{[8_{a}]}+(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{3}\tilde{f}^{(3,1)}_{ij}\right]\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree},[8_{a}]}

(5.62)

where, upon comparison to eq. (
5.39
), we have after substituting
\tilde{f}_{ij}^{(3,1)}
from eq. (
5.57
) and using the
\mathcal{M}^{(-,2,0),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}
of eq. (
5.49
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(-,3,1),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}=

\displaystyle\,(i\pi)^{2}r_{\Gamma}^{3}\bigg[S_{A}^{(3)}(\epsilon)\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}]+S_{B}^{(3)}(\epsilon)[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}]\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\bigg]\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree}}

\displaystyle+\alpha_{g}^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\mathcal{M}^{(-,2,0),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}.

(5.63)

We verify that the contribution of
\mathcal{M}^{(-,3,1),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}
to the colour octet matches the factorisation breaking term in the infrared singularities of the three-loop amplitudes, defined as the function
R^{(3),1,[8]}_{ij}
in refs.
DelDuca:2013ara
;
DelDuca:2014cya
.

In the FL scheme,
the three-loop Regge trajectory is still found according to the definition in eq. (
5.59
).
Therefore, the expression for the FL cut will be eq. (
5.63
) with the replacement of
\mathcal{M}^{(-,2,0),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}
by
\mathcal{M}^{(-,2,0),\,\text{FL-cut}}_{ij\to ij}
of eq. (
5.51
) and it agrees with the results of refs.
Fadin:2016wso
;
Fadin:2017nka
.

5.4.3
Four loops

At four loops the expansions of eqs. (
5.37
) and (
5.38
) in the MRS scheme give

\displaystyle p_{ij}^{(4,2)}=

\displaystyle\,N_{c}^{2}\bigg[\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\alpha_{g}^{(3)}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha_{g}^{(2)}(t)\right)^{2}+\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\alpha_{g}^{(2)}(t)\left(C_{i}^{(1)}+C_{j}^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt+\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\right)^{2}\left(C_{i}^{(1)}C_{j}^{(1)}+C_{i}^{(2)}+C_{j}^{(2)}\right)\bigg],

(5.64)

and

\displaystyle(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{4}f_{ij}^{(4,2)}\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree},[8_{a}]}=

\displaystyle\,\,\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,4,2)}+\alpha_{g}^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,3,1)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha_{g}^{(1)}\right)^{2}\left(\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right)^{2}(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}f_{ij}^{(2,0)}\mathcal{M}_{ij\to ij}^{\text{tree},[8_{a}]}.

(5.65)

As discussed following eq. (
5.35
) the MRS result for
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,4,2)}
is non-planar. Therefore, the only planar corrections to
f_{ij}^{(4,2)}
in eq. (
5.4.3
) come from the other terms, which, as discussed previously, are universal. Evaluating
f_{ij}^{(4,2)}
for the different processes we find

\displaystyle f^{(4,2)}_{qq}=

\displaystyle\,\bigg[N_{c}^{4}\left(\frac{7}{3456\epsilon^{4}}+\frac{43{\zeta}_{3}}{1728\epsilon}+\frac{43\zeta_{4}}{1152}\right)-N_{c}^{2}\left(\frac{3}{128\epsilon^{4}}-\frac{9{\zeta}_{3}}{32\epsilon}-\frac{27\zeta_{4}}{64}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{7}{128\epsilon^{4}}-\frac{{\zeta}_{3}}{2\epsilon}-\frac{3\zeta_{4}}{4}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\bigg]c_{qq}^{[8]}+\sqrt{N_{c}^{2}-1}\frac{N_{c}^{2}-4}{256}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{4}}-\frac{2\zeta_{3}}{\epsilon}-3\zeta_{4}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right)c_{qq}^{[1]},

(5.66a)

\displaystyle f^{(4,2)}_{gg}=

\displaystyle\,\bigg[N_{c}^{4}\left(\frac{7}{3456\epsilon^{4}}+\frac{43{\zeta}_{3}}{1728\epsilon}+\frac{43\zeta_{4}}{1152}\right)+N_{c}^{2}\left(\frac{17}{192\epsilon^{4}}-\frac{217{\zeta}_{3}}{96\epsilon}-\frac{217\zeta_{4}}{64}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 184.9429pt+\frac{1}{16\epsilon^{4}}-\frac{101{\zeta}_{3}}{8\epsilon}-\frac{303\zeta_{4}}{16}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\bigg]c_{gg}^{[8_{a}]}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\frac{N_{c}^{2}-4}{512}}\bigg[N_{c}^{2}\left(\frac{15}{8\epsilon^{4}}-\frac{305{\zeta}_{3}}{4\epsilon}-\frac{915\zeta_{4}}{8}\right)+\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{4}}-\frac{101{\zeta}_{3}}{\epsilon}-\frac{303\zeta_{4}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\bigg]c_{gg}^{[10+\bar{10}]},

(5.66b)

\displaystyle f^{(4,2)}_{qg}=

\displaystyle\,\bigg[N_{c}^{4}\left(\frac{7}{3456\epsilon^{4}}+\frac{43{\zeta}_{3}}{1728\epsilon}+\frac{43\zeta_{4}}{1152}\right)+N_{c}^{2}\left(\frac{5{\zeta}_{3}}{16\epsilon}+\frac{15\zeta_{4}}{32}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\bigg]c_{qg}^{[8_{a}]}.

(5.66c)

In the Regge-cut scheme we have instead

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(4,2)}=

\displaystyle\,N_{c}^{2}\bigg[\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}(t)+\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{g}^{(2)}(t))^{2}+\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\alpha_{g}^{(2)}(t)\left(C_{i}^{(1)}+C_{j}^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t))^{2}\left(C_{i}^{(1)}C_{j}^{(1)}+\tilde{C}_{i}^{(2)}+\tilde{C}_{j}^{(2)}\right)\bigg],

(5.67)

for the pole term in the amplitude of eq. (
5.39
).
Thus, the resulting shift is

\displaystyle p_{ij}^{(4,2)}-\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(4,2)}

\displaystyle=N_{c}^{2}\left[\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\Big(\alpha_{g}^{(3)}(t)-\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}(t)\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\right)^{2}\left(C_{i}^{(2)}-\tilde{C}_{i}^{(2)}+C_{j}^{(2)}-\tilde{C}_{j}^{(2)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=N_{c}^{4}(r_{\Gamma})^{4}\pi^{2}\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\left[S_{C}^{(3)}(\epsilon)-\frac{1}{24\epsilon}S^{(2)}(\epsilon)\right]\,,

(5.68)

where we have substituted eqs. (
5.59
) and (
5.47
).
Then, using eq. (
5.40
) we have

\displaystyle(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{4}\tilde{f}_{ij}^{(4,2)}=

\displaystyle(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{4}f_{ij}^{(4,2)}+\left(p_{ij}^{(4,2)}-\tilde{p}_{ij}^{(4,2)}\right)c^{[8_{a}]}_{ij}

(5.69)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(i\pi)^{2}(r_{\Gamma})^{4}\left[f_{ij}^{(4,2)}-N_{c}^{4}\left(\frac{7}{3456\epsilon^{4}}+\frac{43\zeta_{3}}{1728\epsilon}+\frac{43\zeta_{4}}{1152}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right)c^{[8_{a}]}_{ij}\right]\,.

Comparing eq. (
5.69
) with the explicit results of the different processes in eq. (
5.66
) it is clear that
\tilde{f}_{ij}^{(4,2)}
is non-planar in the octet, as expected. This cut contribution in eq. (
5.69
), after some algebra using eq. (
5.68
) and the two and three-loop cut amplitudes in eqs. (
5.49
) and (
5.63
) respectively, can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(-,4,2),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}=\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,4,2)}+\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\mathcal{M}^{(-,3,1),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}-\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t))^{2}(\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2})^{2}\mathcal{M}^{(-,2,0),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}.

(5.70)

We have shifted all planar contributions arising from the three-Reggeon exchange and its mixing with a single Reggeon into the redefined two-loop impact factors
\tilde{C}_{i}^{(2)}
and the three-loop Regge trajectory
\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}(t)
, rendering the cut contribution non-planar. This will remain true to all loop orders,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(-,n,n-2),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{n-4}\frac{1}{m!}\left(\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right)^{m}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,n-m,n-m-2)}

(5.71)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.0pt+\frac{1}{(n-3)!}\left(\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right)^{n-3}\mathcal{M}^{(-,3,1),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}-\frac{n-3}{(n-2)!}\left(\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right)^{n-2}\mathcal{M}^{(-,2,0),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}.

With the expectation that the reduced amplitude at four loops and beyond will be non-planar (which, at least at four loops, is realised through cancellation of the planar contributions in the pure
3\to 3
transitions with the entire contribution due to the
1\to 3
and
3\to 1
mixing), eq. (
5.71
) shows that the NNLL cut contribution to the amplitude in the cut scheme will also be non-planar. We stress that the freedom we used to define this scheme at two and three loops is no more there at four loops and beyond, so for
\mathcal{M}^{(-,n,n-2),\,\text{cut}}_{ij\to ij}
to comply with the general expectation
Mandelstam:1963cw
;
Eden:1966dnq
;
Collins:1977jy
that the cut is non-planar, the reduced amplitude at four loops and beyond must indeed be non-planar.

6
Intersection of Regge and infrared factorisation

In addition to the high-energy limit of scattering amplitudes, where Regge factorisation and exponentiation are observed,
there is a complementary picture of factorisation and exponentiation which is valid in general kinematics, namely infrared factorisation in fixed-angle scattering. The latter is governed by the soft anomalous dimension.
In this section we will explore the intersection of these two, namely the soft anomalous dimension in the asymptotic high-energy limit. We will use the four-loop NNLL reduced amplitude computed in eq. (
5.35
) to study infrared divergences at the same logarithmic accuracy.

6.1
Infrared factorisation in the high-energy limit

It is well known that long-distance singularities factorise from generic massless
n
-particle scattering amplitudes
\mathcal{M}_{n}
(which we assume to have been
ultraviolet-renormalised) in the following way
Catani:1998bh
;
Sterman:2002qn
;
Aybat:2006mz
;
Aybat:2006wq
;
Ma:2019hjq
;
Feige:2014wja

\mathcal{M}_{n}\left(\{s_{ij}\},\mu,\alpha_{s}(\mu^{2}),\epsilon\right)=\mathbf{Z}_{n}\left(\{s_{ij}\},\mu,\alpha_{s}(\mu^{2}),\epsilon\right)\cdot\mathcal{H}_{n}\left(\{s_{ij}\},\mu,\alpha_{s}(\mu^{2}),\epsilon\right),

(6.1)

where
s_{ij}
are the kinematic invariants and
\epsilon
is the dimensional regulator. Eq. (
6.1
) is the
n
-particle generalisation of eq. (
2.23
). The divergences of the amplitude
\mathcal{M}_{n}
as
\epsilon\to 0
are captured in an infrared-renormalisation factor we denote by
\mathbf{Z}_{n}
, leaving a finite, so-called
hard
function
\mathcal{H}
. The soft anomalous dimension
\bm{\Gamma}_{n}
is defined by the evolution equation
Catani:1998bh
;
Sterman:2002qn
;
Aybat:2006mz
;
Aybat:2006wq
;
Gardi:2009qi
;
Gardi:2009zv
;
Dixon:2009ur
;
Becher:2009cu
;
Becher:2019avh
;
Almelid:2015jia
;
Almelid:2017qju
;
Magnea:2021fvy

\mu\frac{d}{d\mu}\mathbf{Z}_{n}\left(\{s_{ij}\},\mu,\alpha_{s}(\mu^{2}),\epsilon\right)=-\mathbf{Z}_{n}\left(\{s_{ij}\},\mu,\alpha_{s}(\mu^{2}),\epsilon\right)\bm{\Gamma}_{n}(\{s_{ij}\},\mu,\alpha_{s}(\mu^{2}))\,,

(6.2)

whose solution is the exponential

\mathbf{Z}_{n}\left(\{s_{ij}\},\mu,\alpha_{s}(\mu^{2}),\epsilon\right)=\mathcal{P}\exp{\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\mu^{2}}\frac{d\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\bm{\Gamma}_{n}(\{s_{ij}\},\lambda,\alpha_{s}(\lambda^{2}))\right\}}.

(6.3)

Here
\mathcal{P}
stands for “path” ordering of the colour matrices appearing in the expansion of the exponential in correspondence with the scale
\lambda
, such that harder instances of
\bm{\Gamma}_{n}
appear closer to the hard function
{\cal H}
. The anomalous dimension is expanded in terms of the
d=4-2\epsilon
dimensional coupling (
2.25b
), such that integration over this coupling in the exponent of eq. (
6.3
) generates the infrared poles in
\epsilon
as
\lambda\to 0
.
The soft anomalous dimension is known through three loops
Almelid:2015jia
;
Almelid:2017qju
and we present an ansatz for it at four loops in section
7.1
following ref.
Becher:2019avh
, which we then constrain using the high-energy limit.

In the high-energy limit of
2\to 2
scattering, the soft anomalous dimension takes the form of eq. (
2.26
) which we repeat here for clarity
DelDuca:2011wkl
;
DelDuca:2011ae
;
Caron-Huot:2017fxr

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{ij\to ij}\left(\alpha_{s}(\lambda^{2}),L,\frac{-t}{\lambda^{2}}\right)=&\,\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{K}(\alpha_{s})\left[L\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}+i\pi\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\right]+\Gamma_{i}\left(\alpha_{s},\frac{-t}{\lambda^{2}}\right)+\Gamma_{j}\left(\alpha_{s},\frac{-t}{\lambda^{2}}\right)\\
&+\sum_{\ell=3}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{\ell}\sum^{\ell-1}_{m=0}L^{m}\mathbf{\Delta}^{(\ell,m)},\end{split}

(6.4)

where
\Gamma_{i}
is the anomalous dimension generating collinear singularities, defined in eq. (
2.29
),
\gamma_{K}
is the component of the cusp anomalous dimension (
2.29
) proportional to the quadratic Casimir,
L
the signature-even large logarithm of eq. (
2.9
),
i
corresponds to the projectile and
j
to the target. The first line of eq. (
6.4
) is the so-called dipole formula in the high-energy limit. Through two loops there are only dipole correlations, as there are no tripole terms in massless scattering
Aybat:2006mz
;
Aybat:2006wq
;
Gardi:2009qi
;
Becher:2009cu
. All terms that do not conform to this structure are collected in
\mathbf{\Delta}
, which starts at three loops (see appendix
E
).

Instead of using the full amplitude computed in the Regge limit to compare to the factorisation formula in eq. (
6.1
), we will work here directly with the reduced amplitude in eq. (
2.44
). Using the latter along with eq. (
6.1
) we can write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{M}}=&\,e^{-\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\alpha_{g}(t)L}Z_{i}^{-1}Z_{j}^{-1}\mathbf{Z}\mathcal{H}\\
\equiv&\,e^{-\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\alpha_{g}(t)L}\,\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\,\mathcal{H}\end{split}

(6.5)

where
Z_{i}
is determined by
\Gamma_{i}
according to eq. (
2.42
) and we defined a modified infrared-renormalisation factor
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}
, which generates no collinear singularities. This will itself have a modified soft anomalous dimension

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\left(L,\alpha_{s}\right)\equiv\,

\displaystyle\,\mathbf{\Gamma}_{ij\to ij}\left(\alpha_{s},L,\frac{-t}{\lambda^{2}}\right)-\Gamma_{i}\left(\alpha_{s},\frac{-t}{\lambda^{2}}\right)-\Gamma_{j}\left(\alpha_{s},\frac{-t}{\lambda^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{K}(\alpha_{s})\left[L\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}+i\pi\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\right]+\sum_{\ell=3}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{\ell}\sum^{\ell-1}_{m=0}L^{m}\mathbf{\Delta}^{(\ell,m)}\,,

(6.6)

such that the corresponding infrared-renormalisation factor we defined in eq. (
6.5
) is obtained, similarly to eq. (
6.3
), by

\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}\left(L,\alpha_{s}(\mu^{2}),\epsilon\right)=\mathcal{P}\exp{\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\mu^{2}}\frac{d\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}{\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}(L,\alpha_{s}(\lambda^{2}))\right\}}.

(6.7)

Just as the amplitude can be separated by signature, so can
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
. Since
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
appears as the coefficient of the
\frac{1}{\epsilon}
poles multiplying the tree-level amplitude in eq. (
2.5
), which itself is odd,
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{(\pm)}
corresponds to
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(\mp)}
.
We have
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}=\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{(+)}+\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{(-)}
with

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{(+)}\left(L,\alpha_{s}\right)=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{K}(\alpha_{s})L\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}+\sum_{\ell=3}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{\ell}\sum^{\ell-1}_{m=0}L^{m}\mathbf{\Delta}^{(+,\ell,m)}\,,

(6.8a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{(-)}\left(L,\alpha_{s}\right)=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{K}(\alpha_{s})i\pi\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}+\sum_{\ell=3}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{\ell}\sum^{\ell-1}_{m=0}L^{m}\mathbf{\Delta}^{(-,\ell,m)}\,,

(6.8b)

where we note that
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{(+)}
is even under
s\leftrightarrow u
whereas
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{(-)}
is odd.

Upon inverting eq. (
6.5
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}e^{\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\alpha_{g}(t)L}\hat{\mathcal{M}}.

(6.9)

By demanding
\mathcal{H}
to be finite, we can use our knowledge of
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
to determine
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}
. Specifically, we can find the single-pole behaviour of
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}
, corresponding to the soft anomalous dimension coefficients
\mathbf{\Delta}^{(\ell,m)}
in eq. (
6.1
). Furthermore, the expansion provides a highly non-trivial consistency check on the
higher-order poles of the high-energy amplitude through four loops.

6.2
Expanding the factorisation formula in the high-energy limit

We now proceed to expand eq. (
6.9
) to four loops and NNLL accuracy. The expansion of
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}
is not only complicated by it being a matrix exponential but also the path ordering present in eq. (
6.7
) dictates the order of different instances of
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
evaluated at different scales. It is clear that the latter difficulty only starts being relevant at four loops, where there is a term from the interference of the one-loop
\gamma_{K}^{(1)}
and the three-loop
\mathbf{\Delta}^{(3)}
in the expansion of the exponential with
\tilde{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
of eq. (
6.1
) as the exponent. The term contributing to the odd amplitude at NNLL is proportional to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\mu^{2}}\frac{d\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{0}^{\lambda^{2}}

\displaystyle\frac{d\xi^{2}}{\xi^{2}}\bigg\{\gamma_{K}^{(1)}(\alpha_{s}(\xi^{2}))\left[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\mathbf{\Delta}^{(+,3,1)}(\alpha_{s}(\lambda^{2}))+i\pi\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\mathbf{\Delta}^{(-,3,2)}(\alpha_{s}(\lambda^{2}))\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 18.0pt+\left[\mathbf{\Delta}^{(+,3,1)}(\alpha_{s}(\xi^{2}))\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}+i\pi\mathbf{\Delta}^{(-,3,2)}(\alpha_{s}(\xi^{2}))\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\right]\gamma_{K}^{(1)}(\alpha_{s}(\lambda^{2}))\bigg\}.

(6.10)

By explicit calculation of
\mathbf{\Delta}^{(3)}

Almelid:2015jia
;
Caron-Huot:2017fxr
we have

\mathbf{\Delta}^{(-,3,2)}=\mathbf{\Delta}^{(+,3,1)}=0\,,

(6.11)

see appendix
E
. As such (
6.10
) vanishes, so path-ordering does not in fact complicate the expansion relevant for our NNLL analysis of the odd amplitude. We mention though that path-ordering does need to be taken into account if we were to examine the NNLL even amplitude or the N
3
LL odd amplitude, since the non-zero
\mathbf{\Delta}^{(-,3,1)}
and
\mathbf{\Delta}^{(+,3,0)}
will contribute.

To proceed we recall the definition of
K
in eq. (
2.30a
) and define the integral of
\mathbf{\Delta}
, both of which appear in the exponent in eq. (
6.7
),

\displaystyle K(\alpha_{s}(\mu^{2}))=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{\mu^{2}}\frac{d\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\gamma_{K}(\alpha_{s}(\lambda^{2}))\,,\qquad Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}}(\alpha_{s}(\mu^{2}))=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\mu^{2}}\frac{d\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\mathbf{\Delta}(\alpha_{s}(\lambda^{2}))\,.

(6.12)

Explicit results for
\gamma_{K}(\alpha_{s})
are provided in eq. (
B
). Expressions for
\mathbf{\Delta}(\alpha_{s})
at three loops are summarised in appendix
E
and corresponding ones at four loops will be determined in the present section.

We may now expand the exponentials in eq. (
6.5
) using the Zassenhaus formula
Magnus_Wilhelm
, as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}=

\displaystyle\exp\left[K(L\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}+i\pi\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})+Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}}\right]

(6.13a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\exp\left[K(L\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}+i\pi\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})\right]\,e^{Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}}}+\dots

(6.13b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,e^{KX}e^{KY}e^{-\frac{K^{2}}{2}[X,Y]}\exp\left[\frac{K^{3}}{6}\Big(2[Y,[X,Y]]+[X,[X,Y]]\Big)\right]

\displaystyle\times\exp\left[-\frac{K^{4}}{4!}\Big([[[X,Y],X],X]+3[[[X,Y],X],Y]+3[[[X,Y],Y],Y]\Big)\right]e^{Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}}}+\dots

(6.13c)

where for simplicity we defined
X\equiv L\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}
and
Y\equiv i\pi\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}
. The ellipses in eqs. (
6.13b
) and (
6.13c
) denote terms that do not contribute to our required accuracy. The inverse of eq. (
6.13c
) can then be easily found to be

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,(1-Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}})\left(1+\frac{K^{4}}{4!}\Big([[[X,Y],X],X]+3[[[X,Y],X],Y]+3[[[X,Y],Y],Y]\Big)\right)\times

\displaystyle\hskip-15.0pt\left(1-\frac{K^{3}}{3!}\Big(2[Y,[X,Y]]+[X,[X,Y]]\Big)\right)\left(1+\frac{K^{2}}{2}[X,Y]+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{K^{2}}{2}[X,Y]\right)^{2}\right)e^{-KY}e^{-KX},

(6.14)

again, up to corrections irrelevant for NNLL accuracy through four loops. Then, following ref.
Caron-Huot:2017fxr
, we define a cusp-subtracted Regge trajectory

\hat{\alpha}_{g}\equiv\alpha_{g}(t)-K\left(\alpha_{s}(-t)\right).

(6.15)

This definition is inspired by eq. (
2.32
), where we have seen that
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}
and
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)}
are finite.
It is important to keep in mind though, that
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}
(in the MRS scheme
14
14

14

We have seen in section
5.4.2
, that in the MRS scheme
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}
is divergent, while in the Regge-cut scheme

\hat{\tilde{\alpha}}_{g}^{(3)}=\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}-K^{(3)}
is finite
(see (
A.10
)).
, in which we work here) is divergent. Using the definition of
\hat{\alpha}_{g}
in eq. (
6.15
) the hard function in eq. (
6.9
) admits

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{H}&=(1-Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}})\left(1+\frac{K^{4}}{4!}\Big([[[X,Y],X],X]+3[[[X,Y],X],Y]+3[[[X,Y],Y],Y]\Big)\right)\\
&\hskip-10.0pt\times\left(1-\frac{K^{3}}{3!}\Big(2[Y,[X,Y]]+[X,[X,Y]]\Big)\right)\left(1+\frac{K^{2}}{2}[X,Y]+\frac{K^{4}}{8}[X,Y]^{2}\right)e^{-KY}e^{\hat{\alpha}_{g}X}\hat{\mathcal{M}}.\end{split}

(6.16)

We will now expand the left and right hand side of eq. (
6.16
) in terms of
\alpha_{s}/\pi
and
L
. As
Y
is logarithmically suppressed compared to
X
, we notice that
Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}}
, which is a pure pole in
\epsilon
, cannot receive contributions at LL accuracy. Furthermore, the leading-logarithmic hard function
\mathcal{H}^{(n,n)}
comes from only the first term in the expansion of the last exponential in eq. (
6.16
). It is given solely by the one-loop
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}

\mathcal{H}^{(-,n,n)}=\frac{1}{n!}\left(\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right)^{n}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}},

(6.17)

and
\mathcal{H}^{(+,n,n)}=0
. Without knowing the result for
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}
, eq. (
6.17
) immediately implies that it must be finite. Indeed, using the explicit quantities, namely
\gamma_{K}^{(1)}
of eq. (
B
) in (
6.12
) along with eq. (
2.7
), we have

K^{(1)}=\frac{1}{2\epsilon};\qquad\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}=\alpha_{g}^{(1)}(t)-K^{(1)}=\frac{r_{\Gamma}}{2\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2\epsilon}=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,,

(6.18)

which we have seen from eq. (
2.31
).

6.3
Extracting information from lower-loop orders

If we were to proceed directly to the four-loop NNLL hard function,
\mathcal{H}^{(4,2)}
in the expansion of eq. (
6.16
) using the results of
section
5
for
\hat{\mathcal{M}}
,
we would find that it depends on
\alpha_{g}
and
D_{i}
. A priori these quantities are theory-dependent and are found after matching to the full scattering amplitude calculation. However, using lower-loop results we will see that the divergent terms of these quantities are universal (to the required logarithmic accuracy) and do not depend on the underlying theory. The derivation of the formulae presented in this section, up to three loops, follows closely section 4 of ref.
Caron-Huot:2017fxr
.

6.3.1
One loop

At one loop the expansion of eq. (
6.16
) at NLL accuracy gives for the hard function

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(1,0)}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(1,0)}-i\pi K^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}.

(6.19)

Taking the real and imaginary parts of eq. (
6.19
) we gain access to the odd and even parts of the amplitude, respectively,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(-,1,0)}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,1,0)}

\displaystyle=\left(D_{i}^{(1)}+D_{j}^{(1)}\right)\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}

(6.20a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(+,1,0)}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(+,1,0)}-i\pi K^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}

\displaystyle=i\pi\left(\frac{r_{\Gamma}}{2\epsilon}-K^{(1)}\right)\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}

(6.20b)

where we have used the results for
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(+,1,0)}
and
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,1,0)}
in eqs. (
2.46a
) and the one-loop (
2.45
), respectively. We can then deduce that the one-loop impact factors need to be finite. After matching to the one-loop amplitude, they are given by
Caron-Huot:2017fxr

\displaystyle D_{g}^{(1)}=

\displaystyle-N_{c}\left(\frac{67}{72}-\zeta_{2}\right)+\frac{5}{36}n_{f}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon),

(6.21a)

\displaystyle D_{q}^{(1)}=

\displaystyle N_{c}\left(\frac{13}{72}+\frac{7}{8}\zeta_{2}\right)+\frac{1}{N_{c}}\left(1-\frac{1}{8}\zeta_{2}\right)-\frac{5}{36}n_{f}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon).

(6.21b)

Higher-order terms in
\epsilon
can be found in appendix
A
.

6.3.2
Two loops

The expansion of eq. (
6.16
) at two loops gives the following expressions for the hard function at NLL and NNLL,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(2,1)}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(2,1)}+\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(1,0)}+\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+i\pi K^{(1)}\left(\frac{1}{2}K^{(1)}[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]-\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right)\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}},

(6.22a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(2,0)}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(2,0)}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}(K^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}-i\pi\left(K^{(2)}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}+K^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(1,0)}\right).

(6.22b)

We now take the odd part of eq. (
6.22a
) and using the result for the reduced amplitudes
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,1,0)}
in eq. (
2.45
) and that
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,2,1)}=0
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(-,2,1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)}+\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\left(D_{i}^{(1)}+D_{j}^{(1)}\right)\right)\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}.

(6.23)

As
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}
and
D_{i}^{(1)}
are finite in eq. (
6.23
) then we know that
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)}
needs to be finite. For the real part of eq. (
6.22b
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{H}^{(-,2,0)}&=\bigg[D_{i}^{(2)}+D_{j}^{(2)}+D_{i}^{(1)}D_{j}^{(1)}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}S^{(2)}(\epsilon)C_{A}^{2}\\
&\qquad\qquad\,\,\,\,+\pi^{2}\left((r_{\Gamma})^{2}S^{(2)}(\epsilon)+\frac{1}{2}(K^{(1)})^{2}+K^{(1)}\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\right)(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2}\bigg]\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}},\end{split}

(6.24)

where we have used the result of
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,2,0)}
in eq. (
5.12
). Using eq. (
6.24
) and the explicit expressions for
S^{(2)}(\epsilon)
in eq. (
5.13
), and
K^{(1)}
and
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}
in eq. (
6.18
), we find the universal divergent
terms of the two-loop impact factors,

\displaystyle\begin{split}D_{i}^{(2)}+D_{j}^{(2)}=&-D_{i}^{(1)}D_{j}^{(1)}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}(r_{\Gamma})^{2}S^{(2)}(\epsilon)C_{A}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})\\
=&-C_{A}^{2}\frac{\zeta_{2}}{16\epsilon^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0}).\end{split}

(6.25)

It may seem surprising that the collinear-subtracted impact factors contain further divergences. We point out that the coefficients in eq. (
6.25
) depend on the choice of MRS scheme. In the Regge-cut scheme, we define impact factors
\tilde{C}_{i}(t)
, which include universal terms that arise from three-Reggeon exchanges according to eq. (
5.47
). The associated collinear-subtracted impact factors are given by

\tilde{C}_{i/j}(t)=Z_{i/j}(t)\,\tilde{D}_{i/j}(t).

(6.26)

With this scheme choice, we collect contributions factorising in a Regge pole form eq. (
2.39
),

\mathcal{M}^{(-),\text{pole}}_{ij\to ij}=Z_{i}(t)\,\tilde{D}_{i}(t)\,Z_{j}(t)\,\tilde{D}_{j}(t)\,e^{C_{A}\tilde{\alpha}_{g}(t)\,L}\,\mathcal{M}^{\text{tree}}_{ij\to ij}.

(6.27)

It is customary to rearrange the expression above into the crossing-symmetric sum of exponential factors
Fadin:1993wh

\mathcal{M}^{(-),\text{pole}}_{ij\to ij}=Z_{i}(t)\,\bar{D}_{i}(t)\,Z_{j}(t)\,\bar{D}_{j}(t)\,\left[\left(\frac{-s-i0}{-t}\right)^{C_{A}\tilde{\alpha}_{g}(t)}+\left(\frac{-u-i0}{-t}\right)^{C_{A}\tilde{\alpha}_{g}(t)}\right]\mathcal{M}^{\text{tree}}_{ij\to ij},

(6.28)

where
\bar{D}_{i/j}(t)=1+{\cal{O}}(\alpha_{s})
, are
finite
through two loops when
\epsilon\to 0

DelDuca:2014cya
. We compare eqs. (
6.27
) and (
6.28
), finding

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{i/j}(t)

\displaystyle=\bar{D}_{i/j}(t)\,\sqrt{\cos\left(\frac{\pi\,C_{A}\tilde{\alpha}_{g}(t)}{2}\right)}=\bar{D}_{i/j}(t)\left[1-\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{2}\frac{\pi^{2}\,r_{\Gamma}^{2}}{64\epsilon^{2}}N_{c}^{2}+{\cal{O}}(\alpha_{s}^{3})\right],

(6.29)

where we used the one-loop Regge trajectory, eq. (
2.7
) and set the renormalisation scale
\mu^{2}=-t
.

Given that
{\bar{D}}_{i/j}(t)
is finite through two loops, eq. (
6.29
) implies that, by defining
\tilde{D}_{i/j}(t)=1+\sum_{n>0}\tilde{D}_{i/j}^{(n)}\,\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}(-t)}{\pi}\right)^{n}
, we must have

\tilde{D}_{i}^{(1)}={\cal{O}}(\epsilon^{0}),\qquad\tilde{D}_{i}^{(2)}=-\frac{\pi^{2}\,N_{c}^{2}}{64\epsilon^{2}}+{\cal{O}}(\epsilon^{0}),

(6.30)

where the divergence in
\tilde{D}^{(2)}_{i/j}
is fully fixed by the Regge trajectory according to eq. (
6.29
). Eq. (
6.30
) is consistent with our results in the MRS scheme for the collinear-subtracted impact factors at one and two loops,
D_{i/j}^{(1)}
and
D_{i/j}^{(2)}
in eqs. (
6.21
) and (
6.25
), respectively. Indeed, by using
\tilde{C}_{i}^{(1)}=C_{i}^{(1)}
and eq. (
5.47
), we find

\tilde{C}_{i}(t)=C_{i}(t)+\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}(-t)}{\pi}\right)^{2}\pi^{2}r_{\Gamma}^{2}\,S^{(2)}(\epsilon)\,\frac{N_{c}^{2}}{12}+{\cal{O}}(\alpha_{s}^{3}).

(6.31)

Upon dividing by
Z_{i}
on both sides and expanding in the strong coupling, we obtain the coefficients of the collinear-subtracted impact factors in the cut scheme

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{i}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=D_{i}^{(1)}={\cal{O}}(\epsilon^{0}),

(6.32)

\displaystyle\tilde{D}_{i}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=D_{i}^{(2)}+\pi^{2}r_{\Gamma}^{2}\,S^{(2)}(\epsilon)\,\frac{N_{c}^{2}}{12}=-\frac{\pi^{2}\,N_{c}^{2}}{64\epsilon^{2}}+{\cal{O}}(\epsilon^{0}),

(6.33)

where we used the expansion of
S^{(2)}(\epsilon)
in eq. (
5.13
). Eq. (
6.33
) agrees with eq. (
6.29
), thus explaining the origin of the divergence in eq. (
6.25
).

6.3.3
Three loops

At three loops we first see the appearance of the correction to the dipole formula,
\mathbf{\Delta}
.
Expanding eq. (
6.16
) to three loops we will only require the real part of the NNLL amplitude:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(-,3,1)}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,3,1)}-Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(+,3,1)}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}+\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,1,0)}+\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,2,0)}+\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}

\displaystyle+(K^{(1)})^{2}\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\left\{K^{(1)}\left(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]+[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2}]\right)-3\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right\}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}

\displaystyle+i\pi K^{(1)}\left\{\left(\frac{K^{(1)}}{2}[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]-\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\right)\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(+,1,0)}-\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(+,2,1)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{C_{A}\left(\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}+\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)}(D_{i}^{(1)}+D_{j}^{(1)})+\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}(D_{i}^{(2)}+D_{j}^{(2)}+D_{i}^{(1)}D_{j}^{(1)})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-C_{A}^{3}\pi^{2}\left(r_{\Gamma}^{3}S^{(3)}_{C}+\frac{1}{12}\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}r_{\Gamma}^{2}S^{(2)}\right)-Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(+,3,1)}\bigg\}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0}),

(6.34)

where in the final expression we have dropped finite terms and used the results of the odd amplitudes
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,2,0)}
in eq. (
5.12
),
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,3,1)}
in eq. (
5.20
), and the even NLL amplitudes
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(+,1,0)}
and
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(+,2,1)}
in eqs. (
2.46a
) and (
2.46b
). The two terms proportional to
C_{A}^{3}
in the last line of the final expression originate in
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,3,1)}
and
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,2,0)}
, respectively. Using the explicit result that
\mathbf{\Delta}^{(+,3,1)}=0
from ref.
Almelid:2015jia
;
Caron-Huot:2017fxr
, and the fact that the singular terms in the curly brackets in eq. (
6.3.3
) must vanish, we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}+\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)}(D_{i}^{(1)}+D_{j}^{(1)})+\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}(D_{i}^{(2)}+D_{j}^{(2)}+D_{i}^{(1)}D_{j}^{(1)})=\\
&\hskip 20.0pt=C_{A}^{2}\pi^{2}\left(r_{\Gamma}^{3}S^{(3)}_{C}+\frac{1}{12}\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}r_{\Gamma}^{2}S^{(2)}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})=-C_{A}^{2}\frac{\pi^{2}}{864}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{15\zeta_{2}}{4\epsilon}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})\,.\end{split}

(6.35)

Next, using the values of
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}
in eq. (
6.18
),
D_{i}^{(2)}
in eq. (
6.25
), and that
D_{i}^{(1)}
and
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)}
are finite we find for the universal divergent part of
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}=C_{A}^{2}\pi^{2}\,r_{\Gamma}^{3}S^{(3)}_{C}+O\left(\epsilon^{0}\right)=-C_{A}^{2}\left(\frac{\zeta_{2}}{144\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{5\zeta_{4}}{192}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)+O\left(\epsilon^{0}\right).\end{split}

(6.36)

The universal divergence of eq. (
6.36
) is in line with
\alpha_{g}^{(3)}
in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM extracted in ref.
Caron-Huot:2017fxr
and quoted in eq. (
5.60
). Here we use the MRS scheme in the calculation of the reduced amplitudes, as in ref.
Caron-Huot:2017fxr
. In section
5.4.2
we saw that in the Regge-cut scheme such a divergence is not present, as
\tilde{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}-K^{(3)}
is finite (compare
eq. (
5.59
) with eq. (
6.36
)).

6.4
The four-loop soft anomalous dimension and hard function

We are now ready to compute the NNLL hard function at four loops in terms of the reduced amplitudes of the previous section. This will facilitate extracting the soft anomalous dimension at this accuracy. The real part of the expansion of eq. (
6.16
) gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{H}^{(-,4,2)}&=\sum_{n=2}^{4}\frac{(\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2})^{4-n}}{(4-n)!}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,n,n-2)}+\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\left(\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,1,0)}+\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,2,1)}\right)\\
&-\bigg\{\frac{\pi^{2}}{8}\left(K^{(1)}\right)^{4}\Big([\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]^{2}+\frac{4}{3}[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]]\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}+[[[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}],\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}],\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]\Big)\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\text{Re}\left[Q_{\Delta}^{(4,2)}\right]+\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}\left(\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}K^{(1)}\right)^{2}(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2}(\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\Big((\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)})^{2}+2\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}\Big)(\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2})^{2}\\
&\qquad-\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\left(K^{(1)}\right)^{3}\Big(2[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}+3[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\Big)\bigg\}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}\\
&+i\pi\frac{K^{(1)}}{2}\bigg\{\Big(K^{(1)}[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]-2\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\Big)\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(+,2,1)}-2\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(+,3,2)}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\Big[\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}K^{(1)}[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\left(K^{(1)}\right)^{2}[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]]\\
&\qquad\qquad-\left(\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}\right)^{2}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}(\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2})^{2}\Big]\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(+,1,0)}\bigg\}\,,\end{split}

(6.37)

where we have used
Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(-,3,2)}=0
and
Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(+,3,1)}=0
following eq. (
E.2
). Using the results of
K^{(1)}
and
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(1)}
in eq. (
6.18
),
\,\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)}=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})
,
\,\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(3)}
in eq. (
6.36
), the odd amplitudes
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,2,1)}=0
,
\,\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,2,0)}
in eq. (
5.12
),
\,\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,3,1)}
in eq. (
5.20
), the even amplitudes in eq. (
2.46
) and the newly computed
\hat{\mathcal{M}}^{(-,4,2)}
in eq. (
5.35
), we find, up to finite terms

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(-,4,2)}=

\displaystyle\bigg\{\frac{\pi^{2}\zeta_{3}}{48\epsilon}\left(\frac{d_{AA}}{N_{A}}-\frac{C_{A}^{4}}{24}+\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2}]+\frac{3}{4}[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\,\,-Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(+,4,2)}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})\bigg\}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}.

(6.38)

Using the fact that
\mathcal{H}^{(-,4,2)}
is finite, this equation directly amounts to determining
Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(+,4,2)}
. To determine
\mathbf{\Delta}^{(+,4,2)}
using eq. (
6.12
) it is sufficient to use the leading-order expression for the running coupling in eq. (
2.25b
), which yields
\mathbf{\Delta}^{(+,4,2)}=8\epsilon\,Q_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(+,4,2)}+\dots
, so we obtain
15
15

15

This result was first reported in ref.
Falcioni:2020lvv
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{\Delta}^{(+,4,2)}&=\zeta_{2}\zeta_{3}\bigg\{\frac{d_{AA}}{N_{A}}-\frac{C_{A}^{4}}{24}+\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2}]+\frac{3}{4}[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\bigg\}\\
&\equiv\zeta_{2}\zeta_{3}\,C_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(+,4,2)}\,,\end{split}

(6.39)

where in the second line we defined the colour structure
C_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(+,4,2)}
. We note that the latter is entirely non-planar, as it is subleading in the large-
N_{c}
limit compared to
N_{c}^{4}
. This can easily be seen as, according to the argument of eq. (
4.50
), commutators are inherently subleading and that the term proportional to the identity behaves in the large-
N_{c}
limit as eq. (
5.34
).

The finite term of the hard function in eq. (
6.37
) can be found to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(-,4,2)}=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{C_{A}^{2}}{2}\left(\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2,0)}\right)^{2}+\frac{3}{16}\zeta_{4}\zeta_{2}C_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(+,4,2)}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right\}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}},

(6.40)

where we encounter again the very same colour structure
C_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(+,4,2)}
defined in eq. (
6.39
) and
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2,0)}
is the
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})
term of the finite
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)}
. Since the term proportional to
C_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(+,4,2)}
is universal, eq. (
6.40
) conveniently displays the theory dependence of
\mathcal{H}^{(-,4,2)}
, which is restricted to the two-loop
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2)}
.
One can also interpret eq. (
6.40
) as dressing Reggeons with two infrared-renormalised Regge trajectory insertions associated with the Regge pole, plus non-planar corrections stemming from the multi-Reggeon cuts.

We can find
\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2,0)}
for QCD, which is calculated using the explicit two-loop Regge trajectory in eq. (
2.35
) and the two-loop
K^{(2)}
of eq. (
2.30a
) with the values for
\gamma_{K}
in eq. (
B
). It is just the finite parts of
\alpha_{g}^{(2)}(t)
,

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{g}^{(2,0)}=

\displaystyle\,C_{A}\left(\frac{101}{108}-\frac{\zeta_{3}}{8}\right)-\frac{7}{27}T_{R}n_{f}.

(6.41)

Inserting this into eq. (
6.40
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(-,4,2)}_{\text{QCD}}=

\displaystyle\,\bigg\{C_{A}^{2}T_{R}^{2}n_{f}^{2}\frac{49}{1458}+C_{A}^{3}T_{R}n_{f}\left(\frac{7\zeta_{3}}{216}-\frac{707}{2916}\right)

(6.42)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+C_{A}^{4}\left(\frac{\zeta_{3}^{2}}{128}-\frac{101\zeta_{3}}{864}+\frac{10201}{23328}\right)+\frac{3}{16}\zeta_{4}\zeta_{2}C_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(+,4,2)}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\bigg\}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}}.

The
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM result is

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(-,4,2)}_{\text{SYM}}=

\displaystyle\,\left\{\frac{C_{A}^{4}}{128}\zeta_{3}^{2}+\frac{3}{16}\zeta_{4}\zeta_{2}C_{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{(+,4,2)}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right\}\hat{\mathcal{M}}_{\text{tree}},

(6.43)

which is precisely the maximal weight terms of the QCD result in eq. (
6.42
), using the well-known relations between quantities in these theories
Kotikov:2001sc
;
Kotikov:2002ab
;
Kotikov:2004er
;
Kotikov:2007cy
. In the planar limit only the first term survives and matches the well-known BDS ansatz
Bern:2005iz
.

6.5
The soft anomalous dimension in the Regge limit – summary

The soft anomalous dimension in the high-energy limit is given in eq. (
6.4
). In this section, we review the corrections to the dipole formula that start at three loops

\mathbf{\Delta}=\sum_{\ell=3}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}}{\pi}\right)^{\ell}\sum^{\ell-1}_{m=0}\mathbf{\Delta}^{(\ell,m)}L^{m},

(6.44)

where
\ell
is the loop order and
L
the signature-even logarithm of eq. (
2.9
).

Non-vanishing corrections to the dipole formula at three-loop order in the high-energy limit of
2\to 2
scattering, begin at NNLL with a purely signature-odd contribution
Almelid:2015jia
;
Caron-Huot:2017fxr
, namely

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{\Delta}^{(3)}&=0L^{2}+i\pi\Big[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]\Big]\frac{11}{4}\zeta_{3}L+O(L^{0}),\end{split}

(6.45)

where
\mathcal{O}(L^{0})
terms are summarised in appendix
E
.

At NLL, there is a tower of signature-odd contributions to the soft anomalous dimension (i.e. signature-even amplitude) emanating from two-Reggeon-exchange ladder diagrams, which have been determined to high orders
Caron-Huot:2017zfo
;
Caron-Huot:2020grv
(the singularities at NLL are in fact known to all loops
Caron-Huot:2017zfo
). These contributions start at four loops with
Caron-Huot:2013fea

\mathbf{\Delta}^{(-,4,3)}=-i\pi\frac{\zeta_{3}}{24}\Big[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]\Big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}=-i\pi\frac{\zeta_{3}}{24}\bm{C}_{\Delta}^{(-,4,3)}\,.

(6.46)

Note that both eqs. (
6.45
) and (
6.46
) contain a single power of
i\pi\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}
, consistently with the odd signature.

The even signature part of the soft anomalous dimension at NLL is
two-loop exact
Lipatov:1976zz
;
Kuraev:1976ge
;
Caron-Huot:2017zfo
, that is, the terms
\mathbf{\Delta}^{(+,\ell,\ell-1)}
vanish at three loops and above. In particular, at four loops,

\mathbf{\Delta}^{(+,4,3)}=0.

(6.47)

At NNLL, the odd contribution
\mathbf{\Delta}^{(-,4,2)}
is not yet known. The even contribution is given by eq. (
6.39
).
Using the results of eqs. (
6.46
,
6.47
,
6.39
) we can write eq. (
6.4
) displaying all the explicitly-known contributions

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(4)}_{ij\to ij}(L)=&-L^{3}i\pi\frac{\zeta_{3}}{24}\Big[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},[\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}]\Big]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}+L^{2}\mathbf{\Delta}^{(-,4,2)}\\
&+L^{2}\zeta_{2}\zeta_{3}\bigg(\frac{d_{AA}}{N_{A}}-\frac{C_{A}^{4}}{24}-\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}[(\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2})^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}]+\frac{3}{4}[\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2},\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}]\mathbf{T}_{t}^{2}\mathbf{T}_{s-u}^{2}\bigg)\\
&+{\cal O}(L),\end{split}

(6.48)

where
\mathbf{\Delta}^{(-,4,2)}
and corrections at
O(L)
and
O(L^{0})
are still to be determined. The fact that the NNLL corrections in
\mathbf{\Delta}
